. Call the resulting graph
\bm{H}^{\prime}
. Then
\bm{H}^{\prime}
is a configuration model, and we will show that
\bm{H}^{\prime}
is subcritical for small enough
\varepsilon
in the following.

By

Lemma

5.9
and the definition following its proof, as
n\to\infty
, for any
k=0,\ldots,K
,
the proportion of vertices with
current
degree
k
in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,\llbracket{\iota}\rrbracket}
converges in probability to
q_{k}(\iota)
, the coefficient of
\alpha^{k}
in the generating function

(10.21)

\displaystyle\psi_{t_{\iota}}(\alpha)=\sigma(t_{\iota})^{-1}\sum_{m=0}^{K}p_{m}\mathrm{e}^{-mt_{\iota}}\left({1+\frac{\lambda(t_{\iota})\mathrm{e}^{2t_{\iota}}}{\lambda(0)}(\alpha-1)}\right)^{m}.

Now, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,
\lambda(t_{\iota})\mathrm{e}^{2t_{\iota}}\downarrow\lambda(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}kp_{k}\xi^{k-2}
and
t_{\iota}\uparrow-\ln(\xi)
.
Thus, for
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,
\psi_{t_{\iota}}(\alpha)
converges to

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{m=0}^{K}p_{m}\xi^{m}\left({1+\frac{\lambda(-\ln\xi)}{\xi^{2}\lambda(0)}(\alpha-1)}\right)^{m}}{\sigma(-\ln\xi)}=\frac{\sum_{m=0}^{K}p_{m}\left({\xi+\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{K}jp_{k}\xi^{j-1}}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}jp_{j}}(\alpha-1)}\right)^{m}}{\sum_{m=0}^{K}p_{m}\xi^{m}}=\frac{\psi\left({\xi+\hat{\psi}(\xi)(\alpha-1)}\right)}{\psi(\xi)}.

We next observe that
\alpha\mapsto\hat{\psi}(\alpha)-\alpha=\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)/\psi^{\prime}(1)-\alpha
is a strictly convex function with zeros
\xi
and
1
. Since
\xi<1
, its derivative in
\xi
is negative, such that also
\psi^{\prime}(1)(\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\xi)-1)=\psi^{\prime\prime}(\xi)-\psi^{\prime}(1)<0
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}k(k-2)q_{k}(\iota)=\psi_{t_{\iota}}^{\prime\prime}(1)-\psi^{\prime}_{t_{\iota}}(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\downarrow 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(\xi)}{\psi(\xi)}\hat{\psi}(\xi)^{2}-\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\xi)}{\psi(\xi)}\hat{\psi}(\xi)=(\psi^{\prime\prime}(\xi)-\psi^{\prime}(1))\frac{\hat{\psi}(\xi)^{2}}{\psi(\xi)}<0.

Therefore, there exists an
\varepsilon_{0}
that only depends on
(p_{k})_{k=0}^{K}
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{K}k(k-2)q_{k}(\iota)<0
as long as
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. We conclude that, for
\varepsilon
small enough, the degree distribution of
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,\llbracket{\iota}\rrbracket}
is subcritical.

As a consequence, the proportion of vertices of degree
k
in
\bm{H}^{\prime}
converges to

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{k}(\iota)=\begin{cases}q_{k}(\iota)(1-\iota),\quad&k\geq 1;\\
q_{0}(\iota)(1-\iota)+\iota,\quad&k=0.\end{cases}

By the previous considerations and
Lemma

5.9
, for
\varepsilon
small enough,
(\tilde{p}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
\bm{H}^{\prime}
satisfy
2.1
with
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)\tilde{p}_{k}(\iota)<0
.
Moreover, let
\tilde{\psi}
denote the p.g.f. of
(\tilde{p}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
. Then for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(\alpha)=\sigma(t_{\iota})\psi_{t_{\iota}}(\alpha)+1-\sigma(t_{\iota}),\qquad\text{so that}\qquad R_{\tilde{\psi}}=\sigma(t_{\iota})R_{\psi_{t_{\iota}}}.

By
item

8
of
Lemma

10.2
and
, which is proven in
Section

11.3
, (
10.10
) holds.

10.3
Proof of
Proposition

4.2

By the combination of
section

10.2
and
Lemmas

10.3
and
10.4

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\sigma\left({t_{\varepsilon}}\right)R_{\psi_{t_{\varepsilon}}}(\alpha^{\star}(t_{\varepsilon}))-\sigma\left({t_{\iota}}\right)R_{\psi_{t_{\iota}}}(\alpha^{\star}(t_{\iota}))+\sigma\left({t_{\iota}}\right)R_{\psi_{t_{\iota}}}(\alpha^{\star}(t_{\iota}))-\varepsilon^{\prime}+\bar{o}_{n,P}(1)

\displaystyle=\sigma\left({t_{\varepsilon}}\right)R_{\psi_{t_{\varepsilon}}}(\alpha^{\star}(t_{\varepsilon}))-\varepsilon^{\prime}+\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

As
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}t_{\varepsilon}=t_{0}=0
and
\sigma(0)=1
, taking
\liminf_{\varepsilon^{\prime}\downarrow 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}
on both sides yields

\displaystyle\liminf_{P\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right]\geq

\displaystyle R_{\psi_{t_{0}}}(\alpha^{\star}(0))=\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha).

The claim follows from the fact that
0\leq\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)-\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)\leq 2P
.

11
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

In this section, we prove
,
and
in their order of appearance. Taken together, they yield
Theorem

3.1
. While the subcritical and critical cases
and
are relatively independent of the previous sections and given for completeness, removing the finite-degree condition to go from
Proposition

4.2
to
requires some additional thought.

11.1
Proof of

We use the Karp-Sipser leaf-removal algorithm, whose relation to the rank of the adjacency matrix is well-known in the literature
[
4
,
8
,
29
]
, to estimate the rank in the subcritical and critical cases.

Given
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
such that
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}\leq 0
and
p_{2}\neq 1
, we aim to compare the rank of the weighted adjacency matrix
\bm{A}_{n}
over
\mathbb{F}
to that of the unweighted one over
\mathbb{R}
, for which there is a rank formula available. To this end, we perform the following peeling process, which is known as the Karp-Sipser leaf-removal algorithm:

Choose an arbitrary vertex of degree at most
1
and remove it, along with its unique neighbor (if existent). Repeat the process until all remaining vertices have degree at least
2
.

The process stops after at most
n
rounds. In the end, all trees of the graph will have been removed. We call the graph that remains after the peeling process the
pruned
Karp-Sipser core, which is the Karp-Sipser core without isolated vertices.

Let
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS}}_{n}
be the adjacency matrix of the pruned Karp-Sipser core and let
\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}
and
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS},0/1}_{n}
be the matrices obtained from
\bm{A}_{n}
and
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS}}_{n}
by replacing all nonzero entries by
1
. Then
\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}
and
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS},0/1}_{n}
are the adjacency matrices of the unweighted version of
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
and its pruned Karp-Sipser core, respectively.

We next claim that

(11.1)

\displaystyle\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)-\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}^{\text{pKS}}_{n}}\right)=\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}}\right)-\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{\text{pKS},0/1}_{n}}\right).

Indeed,
eq.

11.1
holds true because in each step of the peeling process:

•

If a vertex of degree
0
is removed, then both the ranks of the adjacency matrix of the weighted and unweighted graph will stay unchanged, since we delete a zero row and a zero column;

•

If a vertex of degree
1
and its unique neighbor are removed, then the rank of the adjacency matrix of the weighted and unweighted graph decrease by
2
(see
[
4
]
and
Section

3.1
).

We next show that the size of the pruned Karp-Sipser core is
\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}({n})
. The main idea here is that the local structure around a uniformly chosen vertex in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
is tree-like:
Let
\bm{u}_{n}
be a uniform random variable on
[n]
. By
[
25
, Theorem 4.1]
and
2.1
,
({\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
converges locally weakly to the unimodular branching process
(\bm{G},\bm{o})
with root offspring distribution
(p_{k})_{0\leq k\leq K}
. Let
\mathfrak{T}_{m}
be the subset of all rooted trees with depth at most
m
. Denote by
\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})
the connected component of
\bm{u}_{n}
in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
. If the
(m+1)
- neighborhood of
\bm{u}_{n}
, i.e., the subgraph induced in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
by vertices at distance at most
m+1
from
\bm{u}_{n}
, is in
\mathfrak{T}_{m}
, then
\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})
is contained in the
m
-neighborhood of
\bm{u}_{n}
and thus
\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})
is in
\mathfrak{T}_{m}
as well. In other words, the event that
\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})
is in
\mathfrak{T}_{m}
is determined by the local structure of the graph. Then by
[
25
, Theorem 2.15]
and the local convergence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({(\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n}),\bm{u}_{n})\in\mathfrak{T}_{m}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{the $(m+1)$-neighborhood of $\bm{u}_{n}$ in ${\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}$ rooted at $\bm{u}_{n}$ is in $\mathfrak{T}_{m}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{the $(m+1)$-neighborhood of $\bm{o}$ in $\bm{G}$ rooted at $\bm{o}$ is in $\mathfrak{T}_{m}$}}\right)+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({(\bm{G},\bm{o})\in\mathfrak{T}_{m}}\right)+o_{n}(1).

Moreover, by
[
24
, Theorem 3.1]
, in the subcritical and critical case when
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}\leq 0
and
p_{2}\neq 1
, the unimodular branching process
(\bm{G},\bm{o})
with root offspring distribution
(p_{k})_{0\leq k\leq K}
dies out almost surely.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({(\bm{G},\bm{o})\in\mathfrak{T}_{m}}\right)=1.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{$\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})$ is a finite}tree}\right)\geq\limsup_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({(\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n}),\bm{u}_{n})\in\mathfrak{T}_{m}}\right)=1,

i.e.,
\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{$\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})$ is a finite tree}}\right)=1+o_{n}(1)
. On the other hand, for each vertex
v\in[n]
, if the connected component in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
containing
v
is a tree, then
v
will be removed in the peeling process. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in[n]}\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{$v$ is in the pruned Karp-Sipser core of ${\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}$}}\right)=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{$\bm{u}_{n}$ is in the pruned Karp-Sipser core of ${\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n-n\times\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{$\bm{C}_{n}(\bm{u}_{n})$ is a tree}}\right)=o_{n}(n).

As a consequence, the expected number of vertices in the pruned Karp-Sipser core, which is equal to the number of rows of
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS},0/1}_{n}
(or
\bm{A}^{\text{pKS}}_{n}
), is upper bounded by
o_{n}(n)
as well. Hence, (
11.1
) yields that

(11.2)

\displaystyle\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)=\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}}\right)-\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{\text{pKS},0/1}_{n}}\right)+\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}^{\text{pKS}}_{n}}\right)=\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}}\right)+\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}({n}).

Finally, by
[
8
, Theorem 2]
,
[
1
, Theorem 4]
and the local convergence of
({\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
,

(11.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({\left|{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\left({\bm{A}^{0/1}_{n}}\right)-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)}\right|\geq\varepsilon/2}\right)=0.

The desired result then follows directly from the combination of (
11.2
), (
11.3
) and Markov’s inequality.

11.2
Proof of

In the special case
p_{2}=1
, we remove a small proportion of edges uniformly at random so that the degree distribution becomes subcritical and
applies. More specifically, if we remove a small proportion
\eta
of the edges, the resulting degree distribution satisfies the conditions of
, while the normalized rank of the adjacency matrix will only change marginally.

So let
p_{2}=1
and fix
\eta>0
. We consider the following graph
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
constructed from
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
: Given
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
, remove each edge independently with probability
\eta
. Denote by
\bm{d}^{\eta}(n,i)
the degree of vertex
i
in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
.
2
2

2

Indeed, given the number of edges to be removed from the graph
q
,
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
can be constructed as follows: we first pair
2q
half-edges uniformly at random and remove them. Each such removal yields a realization of the degree sequence
\bm{d}^{\eta}(n,i)
. Then, pair the remaining half-edges uniformly at random. As a consequence,
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
is a configuration model.

Then
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
is a still a configuration model with a random degree sequence
(\bm{d}^{\eta}(n,i))_{n\geq 1,i\in[n]}
satisfying
2.1
with probability distribution
(p_{k}^{\eta})_{k\geq 0}
defined as

\displaystyle p_{k}^{\eta}=\begin{cases}\eta^{2},\quad&k=0;\\
2\eta(1-\eta),\quad&k=1;\\
(1-\eta)^{2},\quad&k=2;\\
0,\quad&k\geq 3.\end{cases}

Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}^{\eta}<0\quad\mbox{and}\quad p_{2}^{\eta}\neq 1.

Let
\bm{A}_{n}^{\eta}
denote the adjacency matrix of
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\eta}
and
\psi^{\eta}(\alpha)=\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}^{\eta}\alpha^{k}
the p.g.f. of
(p_{k}^{\eta})_{k\geq 0}
. By
,

(11.4)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{P}\left({\left|{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}^{\eta}_{n}}\right)-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi^{\eta}}(\alpha)}\right|\geq\varepsilon/2}\right)=0.

Moreover, since
R_{\psi^{\eta}}(\alpha)
is a polynomial of
(\alpha,\eta)
,
R_{\psi^{\eta}}(\alpha)
is uniformly continuous with respect to
\eta
. It follows that

(11.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\to 0}\left|{\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi^{\eta}}(\alpha)-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)}\right|=0.

Finally, by
3.12
, the rank difference between
\bm{A}_{n}^{\eta}
and
\bm{A}_{n}
is upper bounded by
2
times the number of removed edges, whose expectation divided by
n
converges to
2\eta
as
n
tends to infinity. By Markov’s inequality, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(11.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{P}\left({\left|{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}^{\eta}_{n}}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)}\right|\geq\varepsilon/4}\right)=0.

Combining (
11.4
), (
11.5
) and (
11.6
) gives the desired result.

11.3
Proof of

As mentioned earlier, our proof of
splits into an upper bound that is independent of
2.3
, and a lower bound that heavily depends on said assumption.
Indeed, while there is a way to prove the upper bound using
2.3
(see
Section

3.5.2
), by adopting the approach of
[
30
]
, we can in fact remove the reliance on this assumption as follows:

Proposition 11.1
.

Assume that the degree sequence
\bm{d}
satisfies
2.1
. For any field
\mathbb{F}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{P}\left({\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)\leq\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)-\varepsilon}\right)=0.

The proof of
Proposition

11.1
is deferred to
Appendix

F
. The main work of this section will go into the proof of the following lower bound:

Proposition 11.2
.

Assume that the degree sequence
\bm{d}
satisfies
2.1
with a probability distribution
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
satisfying
2.3
and
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}>0
. For any field
\mathbb{F}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)}\right]\geq\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha).

Proof of
subject to
Propositions

11.1
and
11.2
.

The proof is identical to the proof of
[
26
, Theorem 1.2]
subject to
[
26
, Theorems 2.1 and 2.2]
.
∎

Hence, we are left with the proof of
Proposition

11.2
.

11.3.1
Proof of
Proposition

11.2
via coupling

With
Proposition

4.2
in hand,
it might appear that
Proposition

11.2
follows directly
through a truncation argument. Unfortunately, truncating the tail of the probability distribution and re-introducing a finite upper bound on the degrees might have the effect that the second derivative of the p.g.f. of the resulting probability distribution is no longer log-concave – even if the original distribution satisfied
2.3
.
Thus, in the following proof, to preserve log-concavity the truncation, we will follow a slightly different route and modify the probability distribution in advance.

We also utilize the following result that derives uniform convergence from pointwise convergence:

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
(\bm{a}_{n,k})_{n,k\geq 0}
be an array of non-negative, integrable random variables and
(b_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence of non-negative numbers with
\sum_{k\geq 0}b_{k}<\infty
. Assume that

1.

For all
k\geq 0
:
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\bm{a}_{n,k}-b_{k}}\right|}\right]
;

2.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}\bm{a}_{n,k}}\right]=\sum_{k\geq 0}b_{k}
.

Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{\bm{a}_{n,k}-b_{k}}\right|}\right]=0
.

The proof of
Lemma

11.3
is given in Appendix
A
.

Proof of
Proposition

11.2
.

Let
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be a probability distribution, potentially with infinite support, that satisfies
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}>0
,
2.1
and
2.3
. Let
\psi
denote its p.g.f..

Construction of limiting probability distribution
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
.

For each
r\in[0,1)
,
we first construct a probability distribution
(p_{k}(r))_{k\geq 1}
from
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}

that has the property that the second derivative of its probability generating function is
strictly
log-concave for
r>0
, but that is not yet necessarily finitely supported or satisfies
4.1
.

Observe that because
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is supercritical, there exists
k\geq 3
such that
p_{k}>0
. For
k\geq 0
, set

(11.7)

\displaystyle p_{k}(r):=b(r)^{-1}\sum_{i\geq k}\binom{i}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)r^{i-k}(1-r)^{k},

where

\displaystyle b(r):=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{e}^{-kr}\left({p_{k}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{k\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{k-2}{k}p_{k-1}}\right).

In particular,
b(r)\in[\sigma(r),1+r]
, where
\sigma(r)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{e}^{-kr}p_{k}
was defined in
eq.

5.2
. It is straightforward to check that
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}b(r)=1
and
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}p_{k}(r)=p_{k}
for every
k
. Next, let
\varphi_{r}
denote the p.g.f. of
(p_{k}(r))_{k\geq 0}
, such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{r}(\alpha):=\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}(r)\alpha^{k}=b(r)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{e}^{-kr}\left({p_{k}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{k\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{k-2}{k}p_{k-1}}\right)(r+(1-r)\alpha)^{k},\qquad\alpha\in[0,1].

In particular,
\varphi_{0}(\alpha)=\psi(\alpha)
,
and by
Lemma

11.3
,

(11.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi_{r}(\alpha)-\psi(\alpha)}\right|\leq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{p_{k}(r)-p_{k}}\right|=0.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}kp_{k}(r)=

\displaystyle b(r)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i\geq k}k\binom{i}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)r^{i-k}(1-r)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(r)^{-1}\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{k=1}^{i}i\binom{i-1}{k-1}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)r^{i-k}(1-r)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(r)^{-1}\sum_{i\geq 0}i\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)(1-r),

from which it becomes apparent that
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}kp_{k}(r)=\sum_{k\geq 0}kp_{k}
. Then, once more by
Lemma

11.3
,

(11.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{kp_{k}(r)-kp_{k}}\right|=0.

As a consequence,

(11.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi_{r}^{\prime}(\alpha)-\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)}\right|\leq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{p_{k}(r)-p_{k}}\right|=0.

Next, observe that the second derivative of
\varphi_{r}
is strictly log-concave precisely when
(\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha)))^{\prime\prime}<0
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
. For
r\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha)=

\displaystyle b(r)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2r}(1-r)^{2}\Bigg(\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-1)p_{k}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha)}\right)^{k-2}

\displaystyle+r\sum_{k\geq 2}(k-1)(k-2)p_{k-1}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha)}\right)^{k-2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(r)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2r}(1-r)^{2}(1+r\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha))\psi^{\prime\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha)).

Finally, for all
r\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha)\geq\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(0)=2p_{2}(r)>0
. Hence, taking the second derivative of
\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha))
with respect to
\alpha
gives that

(11.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha))}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\ln(\psi^{\prime\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha)))}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\ln(1+r\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha))}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-2r}(1-r)^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\ln(\psi^{\prime\prime}(y))}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y^{2}}\bigg|_{y=\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha)}-\frac{r^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2r}}{(1+r\mathrm{e}^{-r}(r+(1-r)\alpha))^{2}}\leq-\frac{r^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2r}}{(1+r\mathrm{e}^{-r})^{2}}.

Hence,
\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}
is strictly log-concave, which finishes our considerations on
(p_{k}(r))_{k\geq 0}
.

For each
\ell\geq 3
and
r\in(0,1)
, using
(p_{k}(r))_{k\geq 0}
, we next define a finitely supported probability distribution
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
that satisfies the third condition of
4.1
and still has the property that the second derivative of its p.g.f. is
strictly
log-concave. Its definition depends on the support of
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
: If
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
has finite support, we can take
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}=(p_{k}(r))_{k\geq 0}
, while if
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
has infinite support, additional truncation at
\ell
becomes necessary.

1.

(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is finitely supported:
In this case,
we set
p_{k}(r,\ell):=p_{k}(r)
for
k\geq 0
, and check that this choice of
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
is finitely supported and satisfies condition 3. of
4.1
. Let
m:=\sup\left\{{k\geq 0:p_{k}>0}\right\}\geq 3
. Then:

(a)

For
k\geq m+2
,
p_{k}(r)=0
, so
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
is finitely supported.

(b)

For
0\leq k\leq m
,

(11.12)

\displaystyle p_{k}(r)\geq b(r)^{-1}\binom{m}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-mr}p_{m}r^{m-k}(1-r)^{k}>0,

while

\displaystyle p_{m+1}(r)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-(m+1)r}r^{2}(1-r)^{m}\frac{m-1}{m+1}p_{m}>0.

2.

(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is infinitely supported:

In this case, we set

\displaystyle p_{k}(r,\ell):=\begin{cases}p_{k}(r)+\frac{1}{1+\ell}\sum_{j\geq\ell+1}p_{j}(r),\quad&0\leq k\leq\ell;\\
0,\quad&k\geq\ell+1.\end{cases}

Hence,
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
is supported on the finite set
\left\{{0}\right\}\cup[\ell]
. As
\sum_{j\geq\ell+1}p_{j}(r)>0
for each
\ell\geq 3
, also this choice of
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
satisfies condition 3. of
4.1
.

Next, let
\varphi_{r,\ell}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
,

\varphi_{r,\ell}(\alpha):=\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}(r,\ell)\alpha^{k},

denote the p.g.f. of
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
. Our final task is to prove that
\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is strictly log-concave for
r\in(0,1)
and
\ell
large enough. Observe that if
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is finitely supported, then
\varphi_{r,\ell}=\varphi_{r}
for all
r\in(0,1)
,
\ell\geq 3
, and log-concavity was shown in
eq.

11.11
. We thus assume that
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is infinitely supported in the following argument.

For
u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and a
u
-times differentiable function
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, denote by
f^{(u)}
the
u
th derivative of
f
. We then have the following estimate.

Claim 11.1
.

For all
r\in(0,1)
and
u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(11.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi^{(u)}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)-\varphi_{r}^{(u)}(\alpha)}\right|=0.

Proof of
11.1
.

First note that

(11.14)

\displaystyle\left|{\varphi^{(u)}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)-\varphi_{r}^{(u)}(\alpha)}\right|=

\displaystyle\left|{\sum_{k\geq u}\left({\prod_{i=0}^{u-1}(k-i)}\right)(p_{k}(r,\ell)-p_{k}(r))\alpha^{k-u}}\right|\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{u}\left|{p_{k}(r,\ell)-p_{k}(r)}\right|.

We proceed to further bound the right hand side of
eq.

11.14
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{u}\left|{p_{k}(r,\ell)-p_{k}(r)}\right|=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\frac{k^{u}}{1+\ell}\sum_{j\geq\ell+1}p_{j}(r)+\sum_{k\geq\ell+1}k^{u}p_{k}(r)\leq 2\sum_{k\geq\ell+1}k^{u}p_{k}(r).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{u}p_{k}(r)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i\geq k}k^{u}b(r)^{-1}\binom{i}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)r^{i-k}(1-r)^{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{k=0}^{i}i^{u}b(r)^{-1}\binom{i}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)r^{i-k}(1-r)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 0}i^{u}b(r)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}\left({p_{i}+r\mathds{1}{\left\{{i\geq 2}\right\}}\frac{i-2}{i}p_{i-1}}\right)\leq b(r)^{-1}(1+r)\sum_{i\geq 0}i^{u}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}.

Since
\sum_{i\geq 0}i^{u}\mathrm{e}^{-ir}<\infty
for
r>0
, we conclude that
\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{u}p_{k}(r)<\infty
for
r\in(0,1)
, so
Equation

11.13
holds.
∎

Using
11.1
, we next prove that for any
r\in(0,1)
, there exists
L_{r}\geq 3
such that
\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r,\ell}
is log-concave for
\ell>L_{r}
. Since
(\ln(f^{(2)}))^{(2)}=\frac{f^{(4)}f^{(2)}-(f^{(3)})^{2}}{(f^{(2)})^{2}}
, we first argue that
\varphi_{r,\ell}^{(2)}(\alpha)
is uniformly positive for
r\in(0,1)
.
Note that
p_{k}(r,\ell)\geq p_{k}(r)
for
0\leq k\leq\ell
. Let
m^{\prime}\geq 3
be an integer such that
p_{m^{\prime}}(r)>0
. Then as in
eq.

11.12
,

\displaystyle p_{2}(r,\ell)\geq p_{2}(r)\geq b(r)^{-1}\binom{m^{\prime}}{r}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{\prime}r}p_{m^{\prime}}r^{m^{\prime}-2}(1-r)^{2}>0.

Hence,
\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)\geq\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r,\ell}(0)=2p_{2}(r,\ell)
is uniformly positive, and
by
11.1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{(\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)))^{\prime\prime}-(\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\alpha)))^{\prime\prime}}\right|=0.

Hence, by
Equation

11.11
, for each
r>0
, there exists
L_{r}\geq 3
such that for
\ell>L_{r}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}(\ln(\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)))^{\prime\prime}\leq-\frac{r^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2r}}{2(1+r\mathrm{e}^{-r})^{2}}<0.

Uniform approximation of the rank function.

For
\phi:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
differentiable, recall the rank function
R_{\phi}
defined in
eq.

2.3
.
We next show that also the rank function of
\psi
is uniformly approximated by the rank function of
\varphi_{r,\ell}
:

Claim 11.2
.

(11.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{R_{\varphi_{r,\ell}}(\alpha)-R_{\psi}(\alpha)}\right|=0.

Proof of
11.2
.

By (
11.8
), (
11.10
) and
11.1
,

(11.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi_{r,\ell}(\alpha)-\psi(\alpha)}\right|=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi^{\prime}_{r,\ell}(\alpha)-\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)}\right|=0.

Note that
1-\frac{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(1)},1-\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\psi^{\prime}(1)}\in[0,1]
. Again by (
11.8
) and
11.1
,

(11.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\varphi_{r,\ell}\left({1-\frac{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(1)}}\right)-\psi\left({1-\frac{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(1)}}\right)}\right|=0,

and because
\psi
is uniformly continuous on
[0,1]
,

(11.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left|{\psi\left({1-\frac{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\varphi_{r,\ell}^{\prime}(1)}}\right)-\psi\left({1-\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\alpha)}{\psi^{\prime}(1)}}\right)}\right|=0.

The combination of
eq.

11.16
-
eq.

11.18
gives
eq.

11.15
.

∎

Construction of a deterministic degree sequence with limiting distribution
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
.

Given
r\in(0,1)
and
\ell\geq 3
, let
m_{r,\ell}:=\sup\left\{{k\geq 0:p_{k}(r,\ell)>0}\right\}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
.
For
k\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle n_{r,\ell,k}^{\prime}=\lfloor(n-1)\sum_{j=0}^{k}p_{j}(r,\ell)\rfloor-\lfloor(n-1)\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{j}(r,\ell)\rfloor.

Then
\sum_{k\geq 0}n_{r,\ell,k}^{\prime}=n-1
. To make the degree-sum even, we set

(11.19)

\displaystyle n_{r,\ell,k}=n_{r,\ell,k}^{\prime}+\mathds{1}{\left\{{k=0,\sum_{j=0}^{m_{r,\ell}}jn_{r,\ell,j}^{\prime}\mbox{ is even}}\right\}}+\mathds{1}{\left\{{k=1,\sum_{j=0}^{m_{r,\ell}}jn_{r,\ell,j}^{\prime}\mbox{ is odd}}\right\}}.

With this choice,
\sum_{k=0}^{m_{r,\ell}}n_{r,\ell,k}=n
,
\sum_{k=0}^{m_{r,\ell}}kn_{r,\ell,k}
is even and, for each

k\geq 0
,

(11.20)

\displaystyle\left|{\frac{n_{r,\ell,k}}{n}-p_{k}(r,\ell)}\right|\leq\frac{3}{n}.

Finally, for
i\in[n]
and
k\in\{0,\ldots,m_{r,\ell}\}
, let
d_{r,\ell}(n,i)=k
if and only if
1+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}n_{r,\ell,j}\leq i\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k}n_{r,\ell,j}
.
Then for all
\ell>L_{r}
, the deterministic degree sequence
\left\{{d_{r,\ell}(n,i)}\right\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfies Assumptions
2.1
and
4.1
with a probability distribution
(p_{k}(r,\ell))_{k\geq 0}
satisfying
2.3
.

Finally, let
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,r,\ell}
denote the configuration model constructed from the degree sequence
\left\{{d_{r,\ell}(n,i)}\right\}_{i\in[n]}
.
For any symmetric matrix
J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})
,
we may then define the weighted adjacency matrix

(11.21)

\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}(i,j):=\begin{cases}\mathds{1}{\left\{{{\mbox{there is at least an edge between $i$ and $j$ in ${\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,r,\ell}$}}}\right\}}J_{n}(i,j),\quad&i\neq j;\\
0,&i=j.\end{cases}

Depending on the sign of
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}(r,\ell)
, as
p_{2}(r,\ell)\not=0
, by
or
Proposition

4.2
,

(11.22)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}\right]\geq\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\varphi_{r,\ell}}(\alpha).

We remark that even though (
11.22
) was derived for models with fixed vertex degrees in the case
\sum_{k\geq 0}k(k-2)p_{k}(r,\ell)>0
,
\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}\right]
is invariant under vertex relabeling. In particular,
eq.

11.22
continues to hold true. In the following coupling argument, we may thus randomly chose
n_{r,\ell,k}
-subsets of the vertex set
[n]
to have degree
k
.

Coupling the original and the truncated graph.

We next construct a coupling between the original configuration model
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
with degree sequence
(\bm{d}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and limiting degree distribution
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and a configuration model with
n_{r,\ell,k}
vertices of degree
k\geq 0
, where
n_{r,\ell,k}
was defined in
eq.

11.19
.
Recall that we use
\mathcal{N}_{k}:=\left\{{i\in[n]:\bm{d}_{i}=k}\right\}
to denote the (random) set of vertices of degree
k
in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
, and
n_{k}=|\mathcal{N}_{k}|
to denote the cardinality of
\mathcal{N}_{k}
.

Let
n_{k}^{\wedge}:=\min\left\{{n_{k},n_{r,\ell,k}}\right\}
for
k\geq 0
. Given
(\bm{d}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, for each
k\geq 0
, we then choose an arbitrary subset of
\mathcal{N}_{k}
of size
n_{k}^{\wedge}
. This subset will be denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\wedge}
.
In the first step, we construct a graph
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
with vertex set
\cup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\wedge}
. To do so, for each
k\geq 0
, assign
k
half-edges to each vertex in
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\wedge}
. We then pair the half-edges in a uniformly random order, each time choosing a uniformly random neighbor among the unpaired half-edges,
until no unpaired half-edge is left if
\sum_{k\geq 0}kn_{k}^{\wedge}
is even or there is only one unpaired half-edge left if
\sum_{k\geq 0}kn_{k}^{\wedge}
is odd.
For a given matrix
J_{n}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
, let
\bm{A}^{\wedge}_{n}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
denote the
J_{n}
-weighted adjacency matrix of
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
:

(11.23)

\bm{A}^{\wedge}_{n}(i,j):=\begin{cases}\mathds{1}{\left\{{{\mbox{there is at least an edge between $i$ and $j$ in ${\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}$}}}\right\}}J_{n}(i,j),\quad&i\neq j;\\
0,&i=j.\end{cases}

We next construct
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
from
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
by adding the ‘missing’ vertices

[n]\backslash\cup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\wedge}
one by one according the the following procedure, e
until all
\sum_{k\geq 0}\left({n_{k}-n_{k}^{\wedge}}\right)
vertices have been added.
For each
k\geq 0
, suppose that
v\in\mathcal{N}_{k}\backslash\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\wedge}
is about to be added to the graph. We then, one after the other, choose the neighbors of its
k
half-edges in the following way:

1.

Choose an unpaired half-edge
\bm{h}
of
v
uniformly at random. If there is no unpaired half-edge adjacent to
v
,
the process ends.

2.

Choose a half-edge
\bm{h}^{\prime}
uniformly at random among all half-edges in the graph, excluding the half-edges of
v
that were paired in the previous steps and their half-edge neighbors.

•

If
\bm{h}^{\prime}
is an unpaired half-edge of
v
, connect
\bm{h}
and
\bm{h}^{\prime}
.

•

If
\bm{h}^{\prime}
is not an unpaired half-edge of
v
,

–

and there is an unpaired half-edge
h^{\prime\prime}
of a vertex other than
v
in the graph, connect
\bm{h}
and
h^{\prime\prime}
;

–

and all the unpaired half-edges in the graph belong to
v
, break the connection between
\bm{h}^{\prime}
and its current neighbor and connect
\bm{h}
and
\bm{h}^{\prime}
. This renders the previous neighbor of
\bm{h}^{\prime}
unpaired.

Repeat from
1
.

The above construction ensures that
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
is a configuration model, since half-edges are paired uniformly at random. Indeed, our construction ensures that before a new vertex is added, if there is an unpaired half-edge in the graph, it is chosen uniformly at random. Hence, when a new vertex
v
is added, each half-edge, whether already in the graph or attached to
v
, has an equal probability of being paired with the currently unpaired half-edge of
v
. Finally, completely analogously, we construct
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,r,\ell}
from
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
by successively adding the missing vertices and half-edges. Moreover, we use the same edge weight matrix
J_{n}
in all three graphs
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
,
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,r,\ell}
and
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
.

We now upper bound the number of edges that were in changed in the processes of constructing
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
from
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
and
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n,r,\ell}
from
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
, respectively. Observe that each time a half-edge of an additional vertex is paired, at most one previous edge is broken and a new one created, so that in the end, the number of differing edges in
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}^{\wedge}
and
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
is bounded from above by
2\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left({n_{k}-n_{k}^{\wedge}}\right)\leq 2\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{n_{k}-n_{r,\ell,k}}\right|
. Via
3.12
, this implies that the expected rank difference of
\bm{A}_{n}
and
\bm{A}_{n}^{\wedge}
is upper bounded by
\mathbb{E}[2\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{n_{k}-n_{r,\ell,k}}\right|]
.
Similarly, the expected rank difference between
\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}
and
\bm{A}_{n}^{\wedge}
is upper bounded by
\mathbb{E}[2\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{n_{k}-n_{r,\ell,k}}\right|]
. We conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)-\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}\right|}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{4\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{n_{k}-n_{r,\ell,k}}\right|}\right].

On the other hand, by
eq.

11.20
,
Lemma

11.3
and
2.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{r,\ell,k}}{n}-\frac{n_{k}}{n}}\right|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m_{r,\ell}}k\left|{\frac{n_{r,\ell,k}}{n}-p_{k}(r,\ell)}\right|+\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{k}}{n}-p_{k}}\right|}\right]+\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{p_{k}-p_{k}(r,\ell)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3m_{r,\ell}(m_{r,\ell}+1)}{n}+\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{p_{k}-p_{k}(r,\ell)}\right|+o_{n}(1).

Hence, the combination of
Equation

11.9
, the proof of
11.1
and
Equation

11.14
yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{r,\ell,k}}{n}-\frac{n_{k}}{n}}\right|}\right]=0.

Therefore,

(11.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}{n}-\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)}{n}}\right|}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{4\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{r,\ell,k}}{n}-\frac{n_{k}}{n}}\right|}\right]=0.

On the other hand, for any
r\in(0,1)
and
\ell\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left({\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)}{n}}\right]-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left({\inf_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}\right]-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\varphi_{r,\ell}}(\alpha)}\right)

(11.25)

\displaystyle-\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left({\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n,r,\ell}}\right)}{n}-\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)}{n}}\right|}\right]}\right)-\left|{\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)-\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\varphi_{r,\ell}}(\alpha)}\right|.

Combining
eq.

11.15
,
eq.

11.22
and
eq.

11.24
gives the desired lower bound.
∎

Appendix A
From pointwise to uniform convergence

In this section, we prove
Lemma

11.3
:
See
11.3

Proof.

For any positive integer
K
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{\bm{a}_{n,k}-b_{k}}\right|}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|{\bm{a}_{n,k}-b_{k}}\right|}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}\bm{a}_{n,k}}\right]-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{a}_{n,k}}\right]+\sum_{k\geq K+1}b_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k\geq K+1}b_{k}+o_{n}(1).

We conclude that for any
K
,
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{\bm{a}_{n,k}-b_{k}}\right|]\leq 2\sum_{k\geq K+1}b_{k}
. As the left-hand side does not depend on
K
, the claim follows.
∎

Remark A.1
.

Let
\bm{d}=(\bm{d}_{i})_{i\in[n]}
be a degree sequence and
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence of deterministic nonnegative numbers that together satisfy
2.1
. Then
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is a probability distribution: With the choice
\bm{a}_{n,k}=kN_{K}/n
,
b_{k}=kp_{k}
, all assumptions of
Lemma

11.3
are satisfied and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{k}}{n}-p_{k}}\right|}\right]=o_{n}(1).

On the other hand for any
n
, as
\sum_{k\geq 0}n_{k}=n
,

\displaystyle\left|{1-\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}}\right|\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|{\frac{n_{k}}{n}-p_{k}}\right|}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\frac{n_{0}}{n}-p_{0}}\right|}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{k\geq 0}k\left|{\frac{n_{k}}{n}-p_{k}}\right|}\right].

As the left-hand side does not depend on
n
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|{\frac{n_{0}}{n}-p_{0}}\right|}\right]=o_{n}(1)
, we conclude that
\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}=1
.
∎

Appendix B
Continuous-time graph exploration

In this section, we summarize how
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.5
follow from the results in
[
28
]
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.5
.

First observe that for
k=0
, the claim reduces to
2.1
(a). For
k\geq 1
, let
\tilde{\bm{V}}_{k}(t)
be the number of vertices of degree
k
, all of whose half-edges have lifetimes greater than
t
. By
[
28
, Lemma 5.2]
, for all
k\geq 0
,

(B.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|{n^{-1}\tilde{\bm{V}}_{k}(t)-p_{k}\mathrm{e}^{-kt}}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Let
\tilde{\bm{S}}(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}k\tilde{\bm{V}}_{k}(t)
. Then by
[
28
, (5.17)]
, for all
t\geq 0
,

(B.2)

\displaystyle\left|{\tilde{\bm{V}}_{k}(t)-\bm{V}_{k}(t)}\right|\leq k^{-1}\left|{\tilde{\bm{S}}(t)-\bm{S}(t)}\right|\leq\left|{\tilde{\bm{S}}(t)-\bm{S}(t)}\right|.

Moreover, by
[
28
, (5.9)]
,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{\in[0,-\ln\xi]}n^{-1}\left|{\tilde{\bm{S}}(t)-\bm{S}(t)}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Combining (
B.1
) - (
B.3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,-\ln\xi]}\left|{n^{-1}\bm{V}_{k}(t)-p_{k}\mathrm{e}^{-kt}}\right|\leq\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|{n^{-1}\tilde{\bm{V}}_{k}(t)-p_{k}\mathrm{e}^{-kt}}\right|+\sup_{\in[0,-\ln\xi]}n^{-1}\left|{\tilde{\bm{S}}(t)-\bm{S}(t)}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,-(\ln\xi)/2)
. For
t\geq 0
, let
\bm{A}(t)=\bm{L}(t)-\bm{S}(t)
denote the number of active half-edges at time
t
. By
[
28
, (5.10)]
,

(B.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,-\ln\xi]}\left|{n^{-1}\bm{A}(t)-H(\mathrm{e}^{-t})}\right|=0,

where
H(x)=\lambda(0)x(x-\hat{\psi}(x))
in our notation. Moreover,
[
28
, Lemma 5.5 (i)]
states that under Condition
eq.

5.1
,
H(x)<0
for
x\in(0,\xi)
, from which we infer the existence of a
\delta>0
such that w.h.p.,
\bm{A}(t)/n\geq\delta
on
[\varepsilon,-\ln\xi-\varepsilon]
. As a new component is started and
Step 1
is performed only when the number of active half-edges drops to zero, the claim follows.
∎

Appendix C
A versatile approximation

In this section, we prove
Lemma

5.10
:
See
5.10

Proof.

By Markov’s inequality and
eq.

5.16
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{X}_{n}-xn^{\beta}}\right|\geq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right)\leq\sqrt{a_{n}},

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{Y}_{n}-yn^{\beta}}\right|\geq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right)\leq\sqrt{a_{n}}.

Since
\bm{X}_{n}/\bm{Y}_{n}\leq C
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\frac{\bm{X}_{n}}{\bm{Y}_{n}}-\frac{x}{y}}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\mathds{1}{\left\{{\left|{\bm{X}_{n}-xn^{\beta}}\right|\leq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta},\left|{\bm{Y}_{n}-yn^{\beta}}\right|\leq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right\}}\left({\frac{\bm{X}_{n}}{\bm{Y}_{n}}-\frac{x}{y}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle+\left({C+\frac{x}{y}}\right)\left({\mathbb{P}\left({\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{X}_{n}-xn^{\beta}}\right|\geq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left({\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{Y}_{n}-yn^{\beta}}\right|\geq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left({\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{c}}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}{\left\{{\left|{\bm{X}_{n}-xn^{\beta}}\right|\leq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta},\left|{\bm{Y}_{n}-yn^{\beta}}\right|\leq\sqrt{a_{n}}n^{\beta}}\right\}}\frac{y\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{X}_{n}-xn^{\beta}}\right|+x\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{H}_{n}}\left|{\bm{Y}_{n}-yn^{\beta}}\right|}{y\bm{Y}_{n}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\left({C+\frac{x}{y}}\right)(2\sqrt{a_{n}}+a_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(x+y)\sqrt{a_{n}}}{y(y-\sqrt{a_{n}})}+\left({C+\frac{x}{y}}\right)(2\sqrt{a_{n}}+a_{n}).

∎

Appendix D
Crossing half-edges: The event
\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon,n}

Recall
c=c(\varepsilon)
from (
7.1
) and that
\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon,s}
is the event that the number of
j\in\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
such that
\bar{\bm{d}}_{j,s}<d_{j}
is larger than
cn
. By using a second-moment method, we can prove that
\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon,s}
occurs with high probability:

Lemma D.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2-\sigma(-\ln\xi)/2)
. Then, uniformly for
s\in[\varepsilon,1-\sigma(-\ln\xi)-\varepsilon]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon,s}^{c}}\right)=\bar{o}_{n}(1).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}k\bm{V}_{k,\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}-\sum_{k=0}^{K}k\bar{\bm{V}}_{k,\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}

denote the number of half-edges connecting vertices in
\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
to vertices in
[n]\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
. By the combination of
Lemmas

5.8
and
5.9
and the dominated convergence theorem,

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{n^{-1}\bm{C}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}-\lambda(t_{s})+\frac{\lambda^{2}(t_{s})\mathrm{e}^{2t_{s}}}{\lambda(0)}}\right|=\bar{o}_{n}(1).

Since there are
\bm{C}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
edges between
\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
and
[n]\backslash\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
, the number of vertices in
\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
which are connected to at least one vertex in
[n]\backslash\mathcal{V}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}
is lower bounded by
\bm{C}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}/K
. As a consequence, the definition of
c
ensures that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon,s}^{c}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{C}_{\llbracket{s}\rrbracket}\leq Kcn}\right)=\bar{o}_{n}(1).

∎

Appendix E
Proof of
Lemma

10.2
: Properties of
G_{t}
and its zeroes

See
10.2

Proof.

1.

First, observe that by
4.1
and (
5.18
),
\hat{\psi}_{t}
is a polynomial of degree
K-1\geq 2
. Hence,
G_{t}
is a polynomial of degree
(K-1)^{2}\geq 4
. As a consequence, none of the functions
G_{t},G^{\prime}_{t},G^{\prime\prime}_{t}
is constant.
Next, it is straightforward to check that

(E.1)

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha)=\frac{\lambda(0)}{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}\hat{\psi}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\frac{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}{\lambda(0)}(\alpha-1)}\right).

Let
\beta=\beta_{t}(\alpha)=\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\frac{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}{\lambda(0)}(\alpha-1)\in[0,\mathrm{e}^{-t}]
. Substituting
eq.

E.1
into the definition of
G_{t}
gives that

\displaystyle G_{t}(\alpha)=\frac{\lambda(0)}{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}\left({\beta-\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\hat{\psi}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}\right).

Hence,

(E.2)

\displaystyle G_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha)=1-\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)

and

(E.3)

\displaystyle G_{t}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}{\lambda(0)}\left({\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)\left({\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)}\right)^{2}-\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\beta)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}{\lambda(0)}\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)\left({\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)-\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\beta)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)}}\right).

Thus, by our assumptions on
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
,
G^{\prime\prime}_{t}(\alpha)=0
only if

\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)-\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\beta)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)}=0.

Now, since
\hat{\psi}^{\prime}
is a log-concave function,
\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}/\hat{\psi}^{\prime}
is a decreasing function. Therefore,

\alpha\mapsto\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)-\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\beta)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)}

is an increasing function. Since
G^{\prime\prime}_{t}
is a polynomial and is not a constant,
we conclude that
G^{\prime\prime}_{t}
has at most one zero. As a consequence,
G^{\prime}_{t}
has at most two zeroes and
G_{t}
has at most three zeroes.

2.

It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle G_{t}(1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha))=-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha)+\hat{\psi}_{t}\left({\alpha-G_{t}(\alpha)}\right).

Hence, if
\alpha
zeros
G_{t}
, so does
1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha)
. Specifically,
1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))
and
1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha_{\star}(t))
zero
G_{t}
. Since
\alpha_{\star}(t)
is the smallest zero of
G_{t}
, we have
1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))\geq\alpha_{\star}(t)
. If
1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))>\alpha_{\star}(t)
, then

\alpha^{\star}(t)=1-\hat{\psi}_{t}\left({1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))}\right)<1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha_{\star}(t))
, which contradicts the fact that
\alpha_{\star}(t)
is the largest zero of
G_{t}
. As a result,
\alpha_{\star}(t)=1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))
. Analogously, it can be argued that
\alpha^{\star}(t)=1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha_{\star}(t))
.

3.

As remarked earlier,
G_{t}
always has the zero
\alpha_{0}(t)
(which satisfies
\alpha_{0}(t)=1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))
by its definition). If both
\alpha_{\star}(t)
and
\alpha^{\star}(t)
are equal to
\alpha_{0}(t)
, then
G_{t}
has
1
zero.
If one of
\alpha_{\star}(t)
and
\alpha^{\star}(t)
is different from
\alpha_{0}(t)
, item
2
shows that the other is also different from
\alpha_{0}(t)
and hence
\alpha_{\star}(t)<\alpha_{0}(t)<\alpha^{\star}(t)
. In this case, using item
1
, we conclude that
G_{t}
has
3
zeros.

4.

This follows directly from the fact that
G_{t}(1)=p_{0}>0=G_{t}(\alpha^{\star}(t))
and there is no zero of
G_{t}
in
(\alpha^{\star}(t),1)
.

5.

Recall that
\kappa=-\ln{((\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1)+\hat{\psi}((\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1)))}
and let
\beta_{0}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\frac{\lambda(t)\mathrm{e}^{t}}{\lambda(0)}(\alpha_{0}(t)-1)
. Then the combination of
eq.

E.1
and
\alpha_{0}(t)=1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))
gives that

(E.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\beta_{0}(t)).

Plugging
eq.

E.4
into
eq.

E.2
, we obtain

(E.5)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))=1-\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta_{0}(t))}\right)\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta_{0}(t))=1-\left({\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta_{0}(t))}\right)^{2}.

We now come to the case distinction in terms of the position of
t
relative to
\kappa
. If
t<\kappa
, then
eq.

E.4
yields that

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(t)+\hat{\psi}(\beta_{0}(t))>\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa}=(\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1)+\hat{\psi}((\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1))).

By monotonicity of
x\mapsto x+\hat{\psi}(x)
, we conclude that
\beta_{0}(t)>(\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1)
, and by
eq.

E.5
that
G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))<0
. In this case, as
G_{t}(1)=\hat{\psi}_{t}(1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(1))>0
, there is at least one zero in
(\alpha_{0}(t),1)
. By item
3
,
G_{t}
has
3
zeroes. Moreover, by Rolle’s theorem, there exist
\alpha_{1}\in(\alpha_{0}(t),\alpha^{\star}(t))
and
\alpha_{2}\in(\alpha_{\star}(t),\alpha_{0}(t))
such that
G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{1})=G_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha_{2})=0
. As
G_{t}^{\prime}
has at most 2 zeroes by item
1
, neither
\alpha_{\star}(t)
nor
\alpha^{\star}(t)
are zeroes of
G_{t}^{\prime}
.

For
t>\kappa
, using
eq.

E.4
and
eq.

E.5
, we can analogously deduce that
G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))>0
. In this case, we claim that
G_{t}
has only
1
zero. In fact, if
G_{t}
has more than
1
zero, then item
3
shows that there is a zero
\alpha^{\star}(t)
in
(\alpha_{0}(t),1)
.
Since
G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))>0
and
G_{t}(1)>0
, there exist
\alpha_{0}(t)<\alpha_{3}<\alpha^{\star}(t)<\alpha_{4}
such that
G_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha_{3})<0
and
G_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha_{4})>0
. As a consequence, there are at least
2
zeroes of
G_{t}^{\prime}
in
(\alpha_{0}(t),1)
. Analogously, there are at least
2
zeroes of
G_{t}^{\prime}
in
(0,\alpha_{0}(t))
. Hence,
G_{t}^{\prime}
has at least
4
zeroes, which contradicts item
1
. Thus, we conclude that
G_{t}
has
1
zero.

Finally, for
t=\kappa
, using
eq.

E.4
and
eq.

E.5
, it follows that
\beta_{0}(\kappa)=(\hat{\psi}^{\prime})^{-1}(1)
and
G^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\alpha_{0}(\kappa))=0
. Moreover, plugging
eq.

E.4
into
eq.

E.3
gives that
G^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa}(\alpha_{0}(\kappa))=0
as well. Since
\alpha\mapsto\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\left({\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\hat{\psi}(\beta)}\right)}\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)-\frac{\hat{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\beta)}{\hat{\psi}^{\prime}(\beta)}
is an increasing function as shown in the proof of item
1
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
G^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa}(\alpha)<0
for
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0}(\kappa)-\varepsilon,\alpha_{0}(\kappa))
and

G^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa}(\alpha)>0
for
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0}(\kappa),\alpha_{0}(\kappa)+\varepsilon)
. Hence,
G^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\alpha)>0
on
(\alpha_{0}(\kappa)-\varepsilon,\alpha_{0}(\kappa))\cup(\alpha_{0}(\kappa),\alpha_{0}(\kappa)+\varepsilon)
. We can now deduce that
G_{t}
has only one zero by the same line of argument as in the case
t>\kappa
.

6.

Provided item
5
and using the implicit function theorem, the proof is a slight variation of the proof of item 7 in
[
26
, Lemma 6.1]
.

7.

The proof is identical to the proof of item 8 of
[
26
, Lemma 6.1]
.

8.

Recall that
R_{\psi_{t}}(\alpha)=2-\psi_{t}(1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha))-\psi_{t}(\alpha)-\psi^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha)(1-\alpha)
. Since
\hat{\psi}_{t}(x)=\psi_{t}^{\prime}(x)/\psi^{\prime}(1)
,

\displaystyle R_{\psi_{t}}^{\prime}(\alpha)=\psi_{t}^{\prime}(1-\hat{\psi}_{t}(\alpha))\hat{\psi}_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha)-\psi_{t}^{\prime}(\alpha)-\psi^{\prime\prime}_{t}(\alpha)(1-\alpha)+\psi^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha)=\psi_{t}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)G_{t}(\alpha).

As
\psi_{t}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)>0
, the sign of
G_{t}
determines whether
R_{\psi_{t}}
is increasing or decreasing. As
G_{t}(0)<0
and
G_{t}(1)>0
,
R_{\psi_{t}}
obtains its minimum at the zeroes of
G_{t}
. In the case where
G_{t}
has three zeroes, tt is straightforward to check that
R_{\psi_{t}}(\alpha^{\star}(t))=R_{\psi_{t}}(\alpha_{\star}(t))
, and by the proof of
item

5
,
G^{\prime}_{t}(\alpha_{0}(t))<0
. In this case,
G_{t}
is strictly decreasing around
\alpha_{0}(t)
, so
\alpha_{0}(t)
is a local maximum of
R_{\psi_{t}}
. As a consequence,
R_{\psi_{t}}(\alpha)
obtains its minimum in
[0,1]
for
\alpha=\alpha_{\star}(t)
or
\alpha^{\star}(t)
.

∎

Appendix F
Proof of
Proposition

11.1
: Upper bound on the rank

Proposition

11.1
is a special case of the following more general result. For more background on local weak convergence, see
[
25
]
.

Theorem F.1
.

Let
\bm{u}_{n}
be chosen uniformly at random from
[n]
. Let
\left({G_{n}}\right)_{n\geq 1}=\left({([n],E_{n})}\right)_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of finite (multi-)graphs such that
(G_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree with root degree distribution
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
.
Then,

(F.1)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{n}\in\text{Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\\
A_{n}(i,j)\not=0\Leftrightarrow i\neq j,\left\{{i,j}\right\}\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left({\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)\leq\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)-\varepsilon}\right)=0,

where
\psi
is the probability generating function of
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
R_{\psi}
is as in
Equation

2.3
.

Proof of
Proposition

11.1
subject to
Theorem

F.1
.

Since
{\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n}
is a configuration model with degree sequence
\bm{d}
satisfying
2.1
, by
[
25
, Theorem 4.1]
, for
\bm{u}_{n}
chosen uniformly at random from
[n]
,
({\mbox{\rm CM}}_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree with root degree generating function
\psi
. Recall the definition of the random variable
\bm{A}_{n}(i,j)
in
Equation

2.4
. Then by
Theorem

F.1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*})}\mathbb{P}\left({\frac{1}{n}\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({\bm{A}_{n}}\right)\leq\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha)-\varepsilon}\right)=0.

as desired.
∎

Thus, it only remains to prove
Theorem

F.1
.

F.1
Proof of
Theorem

F.1

The proof of
Theorem

F.1
relies on a result by Bordenave, Lelarge and Salez
[
9
]
on the normalized matching numbers of locally weakly convergent sequences of graphs. For a graph
G
, let
\nu(G)
denote its matching number, i.e. the maximal size of a collection of pairwise non-adjacent edges in
G
.

Theorem F.2

(
[
9
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\bm{u}_{n}
be chosen uniformly at random from
[n]
. Let
\left({G_{n}}\right)_{n\geq 1}=\left({([n],E_{n})}\right)_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of finite (multi-)graphs such that
(G_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree with root degree distribution
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
. Then

(F.2)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\nu(G_{n})}{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{1}{2}\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}R_{\psi}(\alpha),

where
\psi
is the probability generating function of
(p_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
R_{\psi}
is as in
Equation

2.3
.

To build the connection between the rank of the symmetric matrices
A_{n}
from
Theorem

F.1
and the matching number
\nu(G_{n})
from
Theorem

F.2
, we adapt Lelarge’s idea from
[
30
]
to our symmetric setting. We start from the observation that for any field
\mathbb{F}
, any symmetric matrix
A\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
contains a full-rank principal submatrix of dimension
\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A}\right)
.

Lemma F.3

(Any symmetric matrix
A
has a full rank principal submatrix of dimension
\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A}\right)
)
.

Let
A=(A\left({i,j}\right))_{i,j\in[n]}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix with
\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A}\right)=m
. Then there exist
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ldots<\ell_{m}\leq n
such that
B=(A\left({\ell_{i},\ell_{j}}\right))_{i,j\in[m]}\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
is a symmetric matrix of rank
m
.

Proof.

For
i\in[n]
, denote by
r_{i}=A(i,)
the
i
th row of
A
. Since the rank of
A
over
\mathbb{F}
is
m
, there exist
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ldots<\ell_{m}\leq n
such that the row vectors
(r_{\ell_{i}})_{i\in[m]}
are linearly independent. The rank of the submatrix
C=(A\left({\ell_{i},j}\right))_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}
is
m
and in particular,
C
has the same rank as
A
. Hence, for any
i\in[n]
, there exist
k_{i,1},\ldots,k_{i,m}\in\mathbb{F}
such that

(F.3)

r_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}k_{i,j}r_{\ell_{j}}.

For
i\in[n]
, denote by
c_{i}=C(,i)
the
i
th column of
C
. For any
i\in[n]
, by (
F.3
) and symmetry of
A
,

c_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}k_{i,j}c_{\ell_{j}}.

Therefore, the set
\{c_{\ell_{j}}:j\in[m]\}
spans the column space of
C
. Since the rank of
C
is
m
, the symmetric matrix
B=(A\left({\ell_{i},\ell_{j}}\right))_{i,j\in[m]}\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
has rank
m=\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A}\right)
, which is the claim.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

F.1
subject to
Theorem

F.2
.

Let now
A_{n}
be as in the statement of
Theorem

F.1
, and denote
\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A_{n}}\right)
by
m
. By Lemma
F.3
, there exist indices
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ldots<\ell_{m}\leq n
such that the symmetric submatrix
B=(B\left({i,j}\right))_{i,j\in[m]}=(A_{n}\left({\ell_{i},\ell_{j}}\right))_{i,j\in[m]}\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
has full rank.
In particular, its determinant is non-zero, so by the Leibniz formula for determinants

{\rm det}(B)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}{\rm sign}\left({\pi}\right)\prod_{i\in[m]}B\left({i,\pi(i)}\right)\neq 0,

where
\mathcal{S}_{m}
is the set of all permutations of
[m]
and
{\rm sign}(\pi)
is the sign of
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{m}
. We conclude that there exists a permutation
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{m}
such that
\prod_{i\in[m]}B\left({i,\tau(i)}\right)\neq 0
. Hence, by definition of
A_{n}
, for all
i\in[m]
, there is an edge between
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{\tau(i)}
in the graph
G_{n}
.

Now consider the directed graph
L_{m}=(V(L_{m}),E(L_{m}))=(\left\{{\ell_{i}:i\in[m]}\right\},\{(\ell_{i},\ell_{\tau(i)}):i\in[m]\})
, where
(a,b)
denotes an edge that is directed from
a
to
b
.
Since
\tau
is a permutation, each vertex of
L_{m}
has out-degree
1
and in-degree
1
, even though
L_{m}
may have self-loops (corresponding to the fixed-points of
\tau
).
The graph
L_{m}
has the following structure: We call vertices
\ell_{i}
with
(\ell_{i},\ell_{i})\in E(L_{m})

1
-polygons. For
i\neq j
, if
(\ell_{i},\ell_{j})\in E(L_{m})
and
(\ell_{j},\ell_{i})\in E(L_{m})
(corresponding to the transpositions of
\tau
), we call
\{\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\}
a
2
-polygon. For
k\geq 3
, if there exist
k
different vertices
\ell_{i_{1}},\ell_{i_{2}},\ldots,\ell_{i_{k}}
such that
(\ell_{i_{s}},\ell_{i_{s+1}})\in E(L_{m})
for all
s\in[k-1]
and
(\ell_{i_{k}},\ell_{i_{1}})\in E(L_{m})
, we call
\{\ell_{i_{1}},\ell_{i_{2}},\ldots,\ell_{i_{k}}\}
a
k
-polygon. Since each vertex has out-degree
1
and in-degree
1
, the vertices of
L_{m}
can be partitioned into polygons as described.

The notion of polygons helps to lower bound the matching number
\nu(L_{m})
, since each
k
-polygon contributes
\lfloor k/2\rfloor
edges to any maximum matching on
L_{m}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
s_{k}
the number of
k
-polygons in
L_{m}
. Moreover, for
f\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, denote by
t_{f}
the number of vertices belonging to
k
-polygons for
k\in 2\mathbb{N}\cup\mathbb{N}_{\geq f}
. Then by definition,

\displaystyle\sum_{2\mid k\text{ or }k\geq f}ks_{k}=t_{f}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}ks_{k}=m-t_{f}.

Since
\lfloor k/2\rfloor\geq\frac{f-1}{2f}k
if
2\mid k
or
k\geq f
, writing
L_{m}^{\bullet}
for the undirected version of
L_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\nu(L_{m}^{\bullet})\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k}s_{k}\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor=\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}s_{k}\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor+\sum_{2\mid k\text{ or }k\geq f}s_{k}\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor\geq\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{s_{k}(k-1)}{2}+\sum_{2\mid k\text{ or }k\geq f}\frac{ks_{k}(f-1)}{2f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m-t_{f}}{2}-\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{s_{k}}{2}+\frac{t_{f}(f-1)}{2f}\geq\frac{m(f-1)}{2f}-\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{s_{k}}{2}.

We now go back to the original graph
G_{n}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
g_{k,n}
the number of cycles of length
k
in
G_{n}
. Since there is an edge between
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{j}
in
L_{m}
only if there is an edge between
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{j}
in
G_{n}
, for
k\neq 2
,
g_{k,n}\geq s_{k}
. Therefore,

(F.4)

\displaystyle\nu(G_{n})\geq

\displaystyle\nu(L_{m}^{\bullet})\geq\frac{m(f-1)}{2f}-\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{s_{k}}{2}\geq\frac{m(f-1)}{2f}-\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{g_{k,n}}{2}.

On the other hand, for any
i\in[n]
, if
i
is in a cycle of length
k
, its
k
-neighborhood
3
3

3

I.e., the induced subgraph of
G_{n}
composed by the vertices of graph distance at most
k
from
i
.
is not a tree. By
[
25
, Theorem 2.7]
, since
(G_{n},\bm{u}_{n})
converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree
4
4

4

We note that convergence in distribution to a constant
c
is equivalent to convergence in probability to
c
.

,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|\left\{{i\in[n]:\mbox{the $k$-neighborhood of $i$ in $G_{n}$ is not a tree}}\right\}|}{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

As a consequence,
\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{k,n}/n=0
for all
k\geq 1
. Hence,
eq.

F.4
gives that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\nu(G_{n})}{n}\geq

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{n}\in\text{Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\\
A_{n}(i,j)\not=0\Leftrightarrow\left\{{i,j}\right\}\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\left({\frac{\left({f-1}\right)\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A_{n}}\right)}{2nf}-\sum_{2\nmid k\text{ and }k<f}\frac{g_{k,n}}{2n}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\frac{f-1}{2f}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{n}\in\text{Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\\
A_{n}(i,j)\not=0\Leftrightarrow\left\{{i,j}\right\}\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\rank_{\mathbb{F}}\left({A_{n}}\right)}{n}.

Taking
f\to\infty
on both sides of the equation above and combining
Theorem

F.2
yields
Theorem

F.1
.
∎

Appendix G
Minor adaptions from
[

]

G.1
Proof of

Proposition G.1
.

[

Fix a dimension

and

. Let

be defined on the same probability space with convex codomains

and
\mathcal{R}_{\bm{X}}\subset\mathbb{R}
respectively, such that
\mathcal{R}_{\bm{X}}
is bounded. Then for any differentiable functions

,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{f(\bm{Z}_{2})-g(\bm{Z}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left({\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{1}}}\left|{f(\zeta)}\right|+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{X}}}\left|{x}\right|}\right)\left({4K^{2}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{Z}_{1}-\bm{Z}_{2}\|_{\infty}+2-2(1-1/K)^{k}}\right)+k\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{2}}}\left\|{\nabla f(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{Z}_{1}-\bm{Z}_{2}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left|{f(\bm{Z}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}|\bm{Z}_{1}}\right]}\right|+\mathbb{E}\left|{\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}|\bm{Z}_{2}}\right]-g(\bm{Z}_{2})}\right|+\frac{2k}{K}\left({\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{1}}}\left\|{\nabla f(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}+\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{2}}}\left\|{\nabla g(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({f(\bm{Z}_{2})-g(\bm{Z}_{2})}\right)^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left({\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{1}}}\left|{f(\zeta)}\right|+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{X}}}\left|{x}\right|}\right)\left({4K^{2}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{Z}_{1}-\bm{Z}_{2}\|_{\infty}+2-2(1-1/K)^{k}}\right)+k\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{2}}}\left\|{\nabla f(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{Z}_{1}-\bm{Z}_{2}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({f(\bm{Z}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}|\bm{Z}_{1}}\right]}\right)^{-}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}|\bm{Z}_{2}}\right]-g(\bm{Z}_{2})}\right)^{-}}\right]+\frac{2k}{K}\left({\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{1}}}\left\|{\nabla f(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}+\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{R}_{\bm{Z}_{2}}}\left\|{\nabla g(\zeta)}\right\|_{\infty}}\right),

where
a^{-}=0\vee(-a)
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

We first note from

that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s}}\right)=\bm{w}_{s},

and from (

) in combination with the assumption that the gradient of

\displaystyle W\left({\bm{\zeta}_{s},\hat{\psi}_{t_{s}}}\right)=W\left({\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n},\hat{\psi}_{t_{s}}}\right)+\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

If further

(G.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s}}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n}}\right)=\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

then (

), (

) and (

) together yield

).

Let
M=\sup_{W\in\left\{{X,Y,Z,U,V}\right\}}\sup_{s\in[\varepsilon,1-\sigma(-\ln\xi)-\varepsilon]}\left\|{\nabla W(\cdot,\hat{\psi}_{t_{s}})}\right\|_{\infty}<\infty
.
To certify the missing piece (
G.3
) under the hold of (

), we apply

G.1
. Taking

•

\bm{Z}_{1}=\bm{\zeta}_{s}
,
\bm{Z}_{2}=\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n}
,
\bm{X}=\mathds{1}{\left\{{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}}
;

•

f(\zeta)=w
,

for

) that

);

G.1
yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}{\left\{{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}}\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}{\left\{{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}}\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n}}\right]}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 4\left({1-(1-1/K)^{k}}\right)+\frac{10}{K}\left({1+M}\right)+\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Letting
K\to\infty
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s}}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left|{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}{\left\{{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}}\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}{\left\{{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\in\mathcal{W}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}}\mid\bm{\zeta}_{s+1/n}}\right]}\right|=\bar{o}_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Then (
G.3
) —– and thus, given (
6.8
), also (
6.9
) —– hold. Analogously, given (
6.10
), (
6.11
) holds.
∎

G.2
Implications in the proof of
Lemma

7.2

In the subsequent proofs, we build on the following collection of results from
[
26
]
:

Lemma G.2

(
[
26
]
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times n}
,
i,j\in[n]
with
i\neq j
and
k\in[m]
. Then

(i)

i\in\mathcal{F}(A)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\rank\left({A}\right)-\rank\left({A\llbracket{;i}\rrbracket}\right)=1

(
[
19
, Lemma 4.7]
,
[
26
,
Lemma 4.1]
)

\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{the $i$th column of $A$ is not in the column space of $A\llbracket{;i}\rrbracket$}
;

(ii)

i\in\mathcal{F}(A)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad i\in\mathcal{F}(A\llbracket{;j}\rrbracket)

(
[
26
,
Lemma 4.2]
)
;

(iii)

i\in\mathcal{F}(A\llbracket{k;}\rrbracket)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad i\in\mathcal{F}(A)

(
[
26
,
Lemma 4.2]
)
.

If further
i\in[m\land n]
, then

1.

i\text{ is frailly frozen in }A\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad i\text{ is frailly frozen in }A^{T}

(
[
26
,
Proposition 4.5]
)
;

2.

for a uniformly chosen
k
-subset
\bm{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq[m]\backslash\left\{{i}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(i\in\mathcal{F}\left({A[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({A[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{\bm{\mathcal{J}};}\rrbracket}\right)\right)\leq\frac{k}{P}+\left({1-\frac{1}{m}}\right)^{k}\frac{k(k-1)}{2m}+\frac{k}{m}\ \mbox{{\rm({\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HofMul25}{}{},
Corollary 4.20]}})}.}

G.2.1
Proof of
Equation

7.7

We restrict to the event
\mathfrak{P}_{n,s}
. On this event,
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
has a zero column in position
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
, so that
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
is not frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
.
Assume now that
i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\backslash\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}])
. As
i
is frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
, we first conclude that
i\neq\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
.
Next,
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
arises from
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
by zeroing row and column
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
, which we break into two steps. By
Lemma

G.2
, on
\mathfrak{P}_{n,s}
, replacing a zero row by an arbitrary row cannot unfreeze
i
, so

i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{;\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}}\rrbracket}\right).

In particular, there also exists a representation
y
of
\{i\}
in
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{;\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}}\rrbracket
. Applying this representation
y
to
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
yields

\left\{{i}\right\}\subseteq{\rm supp}({y\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]})\subseteq\{i,\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}.

Since
i\not\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)
by assumption, we conclude that

{\rm supp}({y\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]})=\{i,\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}.

The just discovered relation
\{i,\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}
is proper in
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
, since any representation of
\{i\}
or
\{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}
could be combined with
y
to yield a representation of
\{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}
or
\{i\}
, in contradiction to the assumption that

i
is not frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
.
∎

G.2.2
Proof of
section

7.3

We restrict to the event
\mathfrak{P}_{n,s}
. Assume that

i\in\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}])\backslash\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)
and
i\notin\{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}\}\cup{\rm supp}({\bm{A}_{n,s}(\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n},)})
. The matrix
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
arises from
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
by replacing row and column
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
by a zero row and column. By
Lemma

G.2
, replacing column
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}
by a zero row cannot unfreeze
i
, so that

i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{;\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}}\rrbracket}\right).

As we assume that
i
is frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]
, this implies the existence of a representation
y
of
\{i\}
in
\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{;\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}}\rrbracket
. Moreover, any such representation
y
has to use row
\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}

(i.e.,
y_{\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n}}\not=0
), as otherwise
y
would also be a representation of
\{i\}
in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
, in contrast to our assumption that
i
is not frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
.
Hence,
multiplying
y
from the left with
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
yields non-zero coordinates in places

{\rm supp}({y\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]})=\left\{{i}\right\}\cup{\rm supp}({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}](\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n},)}).

Thus,
\left\{{i}\right\}\cup{\rm supp}({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}](\bm{\nu}_{s+1/n},)})
is a relation in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
. It is proper, since it contains
i
, which is not frozen in
\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]
.
∎

G.3
End of proof of
Proposition

7.1

Recall the bound
section

7.1.1
. Starting from there, we now follow a case distinction according to the different types.

Case 1
:
Frailly frozen variables
.

3.7
yields the identity

(G.4)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{X}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}=\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)}\right\}.

Using
(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{2})\Delta(\mathfrak{B}_{3}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{4})\subseteq(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{3})\cup(\mathfrak{B}_{2}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{4})
for sets
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{B}_{4}
and plugging (
G.4
) into (
7.1.1
) yields

(G.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{s}-\bm{x}_{s+1/n}|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{K}{cn}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)}\right)\Big)+\bar{o}_{n}(1).

Lemmas

7.2
and
7.3
now imply that

(G.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{s}-\bm{x}_{s+1/n}|\leq 8\delta(K+1)K^{2K+3}c^{-K-2}+\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

In particular,

(G.7)

\displaystyle\limsup_{P\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*}),s\in[\varepsilon,1-\sigma(-\ln\xi)-\varepsilon]}\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{s}-\bm{x}_{s+1/n}|\leq 8\delta(K+1)K^{2K+3}c^{-K-2}.

Since the left hand side of
eq.

G.7
does not depend on
\delta
, we can send
\delta\downarrow 0
to conclude that

(G.8)

\displaystyle\limsup_{P\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{J_{n}\in{\rm Sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}^{*}),s\in[\varepsilon,1-\sigma(-\ln\xi)-\varepsilon]}\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{s}-\bm{x}_{s+1/n}|=0

or equivalently, that
\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{s}-\bm{x}_{s+1/n}|=\bar{o}_{n,P}(1)
.

Case 2
:
Variables that are firmly frozen in original and transposed matrix.

3.6
of firmly frozen variables and
3.7
of the set
\mathcal{Y}
yield the identity

(G.9)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{Y}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}=\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)\cap\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)}\right\}.

Using
(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{2})\Delta(\mathfrak{B}_{3}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{4})\subseteq(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{3})\cup(\mathfrak{B}_{2}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{4})
for sets
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{B}_{4}
and plugging (
G.9
) into (
7.1.1
) yields

(G.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{y}_{s}-\bm{y}_{n,s+1/n}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{cn}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket)\Delta\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket)}\right)\Big)+\bar{o}_{n}(1).

Lemma

7.3
then implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{y}_{s}-\bm{y}_{n,s+1/n}|\leq 8\delta(K+1)K^{2K+3}c^{-K-2}+\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

Now the same limiting argument as in
Case 1
yields that

\mathbb{E}|\bm{y}_{s}-\bm{y}_{n,s+1/n}|=\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

Case 3
:
Variables that are neither frozen in original nor transposed matrix.

3.7
of the set
\mathcal{Z}
yields the identity

(G.11)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{Z}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}=\mathds{1}\left\{{i\notin\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\cup\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}}\right)}\right\}.

Using
(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{2})\Delta(\mathfrak{B}_{3}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{4})\subseteq(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{3})\cup(\mathfrak{B}_{2}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{4})
for sets
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{B}_{4}
and plugging (
G.11
) into (
7.1.1
) yields the upper bound

(G.12)

\displaystyle\frac{K}{cn}\sum_{i\in[n]}\left({\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}}\right)}\right)}\right)+\bar{o}_{n}(1)

for
\mathbb{E}|\bm{z}_{s}-\bm{z}_{n,s+1/n}|
.

Lemma

7.2
then implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{z}_{s}-\bm{z}_{n,s+1/n}|\leq 8\delta(K+1)K^{2K+3}c^{-K-2}+\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

Again, the same limiting argument as in
Case 1
yields
\mathbb{E}|\bm{z}_{s}-\bm{z}_{n,s+1/n}|=\bar{o}_{n,P}(1).

Case 4
:
Variables that are not frozen in original but firmly frozen in transposed matrix.

3.7
of the set
\mathcal{U}

(G.13)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{U}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}=\mathds{1}\left\{{i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket}\right)\setminus\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right\}.

Using
(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{2})\Delta(\mathfrak{B}_{3}\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{4})\subseteq(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{3})\cup(\mathfrak{B}_{2}\Delta\mathfrak{B}_{4})
for sets
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{B}_{4}

) into (
7.1.1
) yields

(G.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\bm{u}_{s}-\bm{u}_{n,s+1/n}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{cn}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)\Delta\mathcal{F}\left({\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left({i\in\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket)\Delta\mathcal{F}(\bm{A}_{n,s+1/n}[\bm{\theta}]^{T}\llbracket{i;}\rrbracket)}\right)\Big)+\bar{o}_{n}(1).

and

Again, the same limiting argument as in

yields

:

Analogous to
